Nabrawind’s Crane-Free Wind Towers Face a Supplier Test

📅 Nov 04, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Spain’s Nabrawind has developed a way to install exceptionally tall wind turbine towers without bringing an enormous crawler crane to the site. Its Nabralift system uses a tubular upper tower supported by a three-legged steel structure. Hydraulic equipment raises the assembled turbine while modular sections are inserted below it near ground level. The lifting frame can then be removed and used on another installation. This addresses genuine logistical and construction problems, particularly at remote sites where specialist cranes are expensive to transport and where wind can interrupt conventional lifting operations.

The company has progressed beyond demonstration-scale hardware. In September 2026, UL Solutions issued type certification for Envision’s 7.8 MW turbine on Nabrawind’s 188-meter tower. Fortescue is using 17 of those tower configurations at the 133 MW Nullagine wind project in Western Australia’s Pilbara region, where construction is underway. Nabrawind previously erected a 160-meter prototype in Spain in under two weeks and has supplied smaller commercial towers in Morocco and France.

The engineering benefits are credible. Nabrawind reports using roughly 20% less tower steel than comparable conventional tubular designs and reducing foundation concrete by as much as 80%, depending on the design and site. Those are supplier claims rather than independently established savings across all wind farms. The potential to avoid large cranes, simplify transport and raise turbines in windier conditions could also matter substantially to total installed cost and construction schedules. High hub heights only earn their keep when those costs are justified by the electricity generated over the project’s life.

The current deployment has an unusual commercial structure. Fortescue acquired Nabrawind in September 2025 and is building Nullagine to serve its own mining operations. Fortescue has the capital, internal electricity demand and industrial incentive to support a technology it owns. Successful installation in that context would be important evidence of construction performance, but it would not establish that an unrelated wind developer can finance and maintain the same system under ordinary third-party commercial arrangements.

Nabralift’s proprietary construction approach creates a procurement issue. Conventional tubular towers can be sourced from several qualified manufacturers, and experienced contractors can compete for much of their construction work. A developer selecting Nabralift currently depends on one supplier for the core tower system, specialist lifting knowledge and associated support. Wind turbine original-equipment manufacturers also create supplier dependencies, but concentrating another critical part of the project in a proprietary installation system requires its own contract and risk assessment.

A type certificate provides valuable technical assurance about the certified turbine-and-tower configuration. A lender or insurer still needs enforceable delivery schedules, long-term warranties, repair and maintenance provisions, clarity on replacement parts, and a workable remedy if the supplier’s capacity or commercial priorities change. These questions become more important when a wind farm must operate for 25 years or longer and there is no qualified alternative provider of the same system.

Nabrawind’s next important proof point is an independently developed and financed large wind farm that accepts those risks on commercial terms. Qualified installation partners, credible long-term service arrangements and greater competitive capacity could make its engineering advantages easier for developers to buy. The 188-meter tower has earned technical attention; its broader market depends on financing and procurement confidence catching up with the machinery.

My full TFIE Strategy Briefing assessment examines Nabrawind’s self-erecting design, Fortescue’s Nullagine deployment and the supplier concentration that independent wind developers would have to manage. It follows my earlier assessment of 300-meter wind towers, where construction economics were the central challenge.

The Dow soars 1,500 points as a Democratic midterm election sweep lifts markets

📅 Nov 04, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Free daily briefing on global business news.

Oil prices fell more than 7% and Nvidia stock dropped 3% as the S&P 500 gained 1.58% and the Nasdaq rose 1.44%

The Dow Jones Industrial Average closed up 1,569.40 points, or 3.03%, at 53,356.12 on Wednesday, a day after Democrats won majorities in the House and the Senate in the midterm elections.

The S&P 500 rose 1.58% to 7,688.62, and the Nasdaq $NDAQ Composite gained 1.44% to 25,736.51. It was the Dow's best day since April 9, 2025, according to CNN and CNBC. CNN, citing CFRA Research data, also reported that the gain was the Dow's largest single-day move after a midterm election since 1982. The VIX, a gauge of expected volatility, fell almost 20%, and bond yields declined.

President Donald Trump told The New York Times that he was "absolutely willing" to go along with the outcome. In remarks to The Wall Street Journal, he said he and Republicans were prepared to cooperate with Democrats. The rebound was partly a response to the tone Trump struck at first, according to CNBC, which quoted Jay Woods, chief market strategist at Freedom Capital Markets.

Stocks tied to the broader economy led the gains. Walmart $WMT stock rose 3% and Bank of America $BAC stock rose about 4%.

Oil prices dropped after Vice President JD Vance blamed the war with Iran for Republican losses in a post on X $TWTR. "The reason we lost is the war," Vance wrote. Brent crude settled at $94.62 a barrel, down almost 8.7%. West Texas Intermediate fell more than 7% to about $88.60 a barrel.

Technology stocks split. Nvidia $NVDA stock fell 3%, and CoreWeave stock fell about 10%. Shares of Nebius and Tesla $TSLA fell 6.7% and 3.3%, respectively. Amazon $AMZN stock climbed as much as 8.3% and Microsoft $MSFT stock as much as 7% during the session, as investors appeared to wager that a Democratic Congress would limit AI spending.

Investors had been concerned ahead of the vote that a Democratic sweep might bring tougher curbs on data centers, according to CNBC. Gina Hinojosa, who defeated Republican Gov. Greg Abbott $ABT in Texas, has called for ending data center development in the state. Republicans hold the majority in the Texas Legislature, which limits how much she can change state policy.

Dan Ives, a partner at Yorkville Ives & Co., said the Democratic gains would hold back AI infrastructure construction across America for the next few years, according to ABC News.

OpenAI is already trying to shape the 2028 election

📅 Nov 04, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Buy One Founder Summit Ticket, Get a Second for Free. Register now.

Founder Summit begins Nov. 4, 9:00 a.m. ET. Get your pass savings & deals before it starts. REGISTER HERE.

OpenAI’s chief global affairs officer Chris Lehane thinks the 2028 presidential election could be a defining moment for AI. And he wants politicians to start developing policies that address Americans’ concerns about the technology, rather than simply paying lip service to anti-AI sentiment and the backlash against data centers.

In a new essay, Lehane compares AI’s potential to reshape the economy to President Franklin D. Roosevelt’s New Deal, the 1930s set of federal programs and reforms enacted after industrialization and the Great Depression shattered the old economic order. He observes that Americans today feel similarly anxious about losing control over their economic futures and urges politicians to find a “compelling third path” that allows people to share in AI’s benefits. He lays out five policy priorities, spanning worker protections and education to AI safety and data center development.

The essay comes on the heels of a stunning Democratic victory in the midterm elections, in which Democrats flipped control of Congress amid widespread voter frustration over the cost of living. Kate Brennan, who leads AI and corporate power policy at the AI Now Institute, told TechCrunch that Lehane’s rhetoric marks a notable shift from OpenAI’s policy agenda last year, which focused on accelerating AI development, reducing regulatory barriers, and expanding infrastructure.

Unsurprisingly, many of Lehane’s proposals would encourage wider AI adoption and support the infrastructure expansion companies like OpenAI need to grow. The original New Deal established protections for workers and placed constraints on corporate power. It’s less clear how Lehane’s vision would hold AI companies accountable for the economic disruption their technology could create.

“Invoking a New Deal metaphor is to invoke state capacity to actually benefit the public,” Brennan said. “This is written by a private company that is trying to advocate for policies for its own benefit. It is ahistorical at best and harmful at worst.”

While AI wasn’t a dominant issue in the midterms, opposition to data centers emerged as a major bipartisan concern in several races in the midterms. And Lehane may be right that AI will become a bigger part of the conversation over the next two years.

Affordability is already shaping up as a key issue for the 2028 election as Americans grapple with inflation, tariffs, and the rising cost of living. AI could become an increasingly large part of that conversation as voters stress about data centers hiking up electricity bills, AI replacing their jobs, and technology’s economic gains flowing only to a handful of wealthy companies.

Lehane acknowledges many of these concerns, but he offers few concrete proposals to prevent AI’s gains from becoming concentrated. Instead, he largely pitches AI as a path to greater prosperity for Americans willing to embrace it.

Take his proposals for the tax code. He suggests incentives for workers to learn AI skills, easier capital access for entrepreneurs, and new approaches to employee ownership and profit-sharing. Those are not the same as binding requirements that would ensure workers share in AI-generated wealth or that companies pay the costs of displacing workers.

“I was struck by how much of this policy is for a future…that is already predicated on mass AI adoption and AI benefiting society, which we have yet to see,” Brennan said. “All of it is predicated on benefiting from AI’s upside.”

Brennan said it would make more sense to see things like a revised corporate tax rate or tax incentives for hiring human beings rather than automating jobs, as well as ways to keep companies from amassing outsize market power.

Lehane’s emphasis on expanding AI’s economic opportunities, rather than limiting potential harms, extends to data centers. He notes concerns about data centers while emphasizing the construction jobs and local tax revenue they could generate. He then warns that politicians who support a data center moratorium could jeopardize America’s lead over China, going so far as to compare that stance to the 2002 vote to authorize the Iraq war.

Lehane also makes child safety and education central to his proposed agenda, stressing the importance of age-appropriate protections and safeguards around AI companions for minors. He also warns against keeping AI out of classrooms, arguing that doing so could lead to inequality between wealthy students and “children in rural communities.” But the argument takes for granted that AI improves learning, a claim that remains unproven and one critics say deserves more examination than more funding or individualized attention from human teachers.

Lehane also calls for enforceable AI safety standards, independent evaluations, public oversight, and the ability to restrict AI systems or use a kill switch to shut them down — though recent studies show that OpenAI and other AI labs haven’t published comprehensive plans for what they would do if a model escaped human control.

Brennan called these stances “disingenuous,” pointing to OpenAI’s history of opposing legislation designed to impose such measures. In 2024, for example, OpenAI opposed California’s AI safety bill, SB 1047, which would have mandated catastrophic risk evaluations and shutdown capabilities. Last year, OpenAI also sought to limit state regulation, arguing that states could undermine American innovation. The company has more recently shifted some of those positions, backing several state safety laws and saying it would be open to independent evaluators embedded in the company.

Lehane also argues that people should maintain control over consequential decisions made using AI, rather than handing off judgment entirely to machines. But that raises the question of what human control really looks like in a society where humans are increasingly being required to use AI in education, work, healthcare, and government.

Throughout his essay, Lehane frames AI as a technology that could give Americans back their sense of agency and economic opportunity. But he ties average Americans’ “middle class” success to participation in an economy increasingly shaped by companies like OpenAI. Whether his policies will meaningfully redistribute power or subtly entrench it is less clear.

TechCrunch reached out to OpenAI for more specifics on Lehane’s proposals and the company’s stances, but it did not respond by the time of publication.

Celtic unwanted 'world record' quip, best starting XI admission and player who might cry if he scores

📅 Nov 04, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Celtic host Besiktas on Guy Fawkes Night hoping to rediscover the fireworks of exactly two years ago when a Champions League masterclass inflicted a 3-1 defeat on RB Leipzig.

The decline of the Parkhead club in Europe since that run to the knockout stages that saw them come within seconds of taking Bayern Munich to extra-time for a place in the last 16 has been well documented.

Did you know with a Digital Subscription to The Scotsman, you can get unlimited access to the website including our premium content, as well as benefiting from fewer ads, loyalty rewards and much more.

It should also be noted that such success on the continent is the exception as Celtic's two previous November 5 results in Europe will testify, namely a 4-1 home defeat by Sparta Prague in 2020 and a 2-1 home loss to Molde in 2015.

As bonfires are lit across the country, Celtic's focus will be on preventing their Europa League flame from burning out as they go in search of their first points of the campaign after successive defeats by Ferencvaros, Benfica and Celta Vigo.

One of only four teams still on zero points after three matches, Martin O'Neill's side are only being kept off the bottom of the 36-team table due to the fact that minnows Ararat Armenia have a worse goal difference.

There are still 15 points left to play for so all is not lost, but the recovery needs to start now.

“I was told that any team who’s lost their first three games haven’t made it through, so it doesn’t augur brilliantly for us if that’s the case,” O’Neill said during his pre-match press conference on Wednesday.

“So if we go on for a fourth one, then that would be a world record.

“We’re definitely running out of matches, no question about it. But we are capable of still qualifying if, let’s say, 10 points is the mark.

”It means you really have to go away and win, not draw."

There are six survivors from the Celtic team that played in that statement win over Leipzig. Alistair Johnston, Cameron Carter-Vickers, Auston Trusty and Callum McGregor started while Liam Scales and Hyun-Jun Yang appeared off the bench.

Johnston and Yang are both unavailable although the latter has returned to training and could return as soon as Sunday, when the Scottish champions host Kilmarnock in the Premiership.

After their weekend 2-0 defeat at Falkirk, Celtic have won just three of their last 10 matches in all competitions and O'Neill admits he is still unsure of his best starting XI.

"I keep getting asked this question and until I know exactly what everyone can do at the football club, then I probably won't know that," he said. "It's not always knowing about your best team because we've had a lot of games and we're rotating a bit as well to try and save some legs, save a little bit of energy.

"I think it's our ninth game in about 20 odd days. We're in these competitions and we're trying to do our best.

"Once each player has had an opportunity, for instance [Jordan] Latomba hasn't really had a proper opportunity, and once players get a couple of matches then you can make a decision. But your best team might be for a certain game and your best team for European competition might be something else."

Former Celtic striker Oh Hyeon-gyu returns to Parkhead for the first time since his 2024 summer departure. The South Korea international netted 12 goals in 18 months in Glasgow but only started six of his 47 appearances.

After being signed under Ange Postecoglou, he found game time increasingly hard to come by under Brendan Rodgers. But a move to Genk in the summer of 2024 allowed him to move his career forward and he joined Besiktas in January for a fee of around £12million.

Speaking in English at a pre-match media conference, Oh said: “I’m so excited. It’s very special for me to be back here.

“I couldn’t speak English properly back then but everyone welcomed me like family here. And Celtic became like my second home. Maybe I will be emotional if I score but I will give everything for Besiktas.”

Reflecting on his time in Scotland, Oh added: “I came here as a young boy with a dream. It was sometimes hard for me, sometimes good for me.

“I have got good memories here. I always be grateful for everything and the fans. If I score, maybe I will cry. I learned a lot about my position from Kyogo Furuhashi and Adam Idah.

“In my last season here, I didn’t play for the last six months so it was a hard time for me. But I’m very happy to be back here because I love Celtic so much. It has a special place in my heart.

“After I left Celtic, it was good motivation for me. I wanted to show what I could do and how much I have improved. Finally I am back here and I have to show them.”

Every stock that moved because of the election

📅 2026-11-04  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

The blue wave across the country has rippled through the stock market too, driving healthcare and clean energy names higher and data center plays and defense companies tumbling.

The broader market soared after Democrats won control of both the House of Representatives and the Senate, and key gubernatorial races in Texas and Georgia. In Washington, the Democratic Congress will now serve as a counterweight to President Donald Trump's agenda, which has included tax cuts and policies that sent inflation, like tariffs and the Iran war.

Local and state elections will also have an impact on data center stocks, with construction limits passed in some states and a Democratic governor-elect in Texas who promised a moratorium.

"Democratic control could slow the push for deregulation in financials, energy and digital assets," Wells Fargo Investment Institute said in a note Wednesday. "We still believe both parties have an incentive to compromise on data center location and AI generally."

Here are some of the biggest movers as a result of the election outcome.

Nextpower jumped more than 11.2 % and Canadian Solar rallied 6.7%, while Plug Power gained 3.6%, First Solar climbed 4.6% and Sunrun ticked 1.3% higher. Nextera Energy added 4.9%. Investors are hoping that Democrats will push back against the Trump administration's rejection of clean energy policies.

Healthcare companies rallied on investor optimism over a slowdown or reversal in healthcare spending cuts, and the potential that enhanced subsidies will extended in Affordable Care Act plans.

Molina Healthcare and Centene gained 8.9% and 12.6%, respectively. HCA Healthcare soared 10.3% and Universal Health Services jumped 10.9%, while Tenet Healthcare rallied 9.6% and Elevance Health added 4.9%.

Elon Musk's SpaceX and Tesla shed 3.8% and 3.3%, respectively, after Democrats won the gubernatorial, Senate, and attorney general races in Texas, as well as wresting control of the Texas House of Representatives . Musk, CEO of both companies, spent at least $474 million backing Republican candidates in the midterms, including $224 million in the Lone Star State, where both Tesla and SpaceX are headquartered.

Defense companies could face headwinds if Democrats challenge military spending. Shares of RTX slipped 2.4%, Lockheed Martin tumbled 6.9%, Northrop Grumman sank 4.5% and Howmet Aerospace fell 4.3%. Palantir Technologies shed 2.1%.

The Trump administration has requested $1.5 trillion for national defense in fiscal 2027. It is also separately asking for an $87.6 billion supplemental appropriation from Congress for fiscal 2026, largely due to the Iran conflict.

Drone stocks also fell. Kratos Defense & Security Solutions dropped 12.3%, while AeroVironment lost 8.3% and Red Cat fell 10.4%. Ondas shed 7% and Unusual Machines tumbled 9%.

Names connected with data centers moved lower amid concern about the potential for restrictions or moratoriums being placed on AI infrastructure buildouts.

Neocloud provider CoreWeave, the poster child for fears over massive AI-related debt, slumped 9.9%. Peers Nebius and Iren dropped 6.7% and 11.2%, respectively.

AI infrastructure stocks also tumbled, with Comfort Systems losing 15.1% and Vistra sliding 13.8%. Micron Technology fell 7%.

That said, five of the Magnificent Seven rallied. Apple rose 5.3% and Microsoft jumped 6.7%, with Apple touching an all-time high. Alphabet gained 3.2% and Meta added 1.4%.

A consumer relief trade also took hold, sending housing, travel and consumer staples stocks higher. Affordability was a big theme in the election, with Americans feeling the pressure of high prices.

Housing stocks like Pulte, D.R. Horton and Lennar gained roughly 7.2%, 8.1% and 7.1%, respectively. Airlines also moved higher, with United Airlines rising 8% and American Airlines climbing 6.3%. Southwest Airlines jumped 6.8% and Delta Air Lines climbed 7.2%. Cruise operator Carnival increased 7.3%, while Royal Caribbean and Norwegian Cruise Line added 6.4% and 6.8%, respectively. Booking Holdings rallied 9.8%.

Consumer staple names also caught a bid. Dollar Tree soared 9.6%, Walmart gained 3.5% and Constellation Brands climbed 5.3%. Dollar General advanced 8.5%, while Conagra Brands increased 7%.

The GOP's wide losses also extended dragged down President Donald Trump's social media and tech company, which plunged 24.5% and hit a 52-week low.

— CNBC's Luke Fountain, Dominic Chu and Nicholas Wells contributed reporting.

Big tech stocks soar as investors bet Democrats could bring AI spending back to reality

📅 Nov 04, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Free daily briefing on global business news.

Amazon, Microsoft, Google, Meta, and Apple were on pace to gain close to $1 trillion in market camp in a day

Amazon $AMZN stock climbed as much as 8.3% on Wednesday, with investors appearing to wager that a Democratic-controlled Congress will rein in AI spending that is draining free cash flow.

Microsoft $MSFT stock gained as much as 7%, Alphabet $GOOGL stock rose as much as 5.9% and Meta $META Platforms stock advanced as much as 3.6%. Apple $AAPL stock rose more than 6%. Apple has steered clear of big outlays on AI,. Together, the five companies are on pace to gain close to $1 trillion in market capitalization on Wednesday.

Nvidia $NVDA stock and Tesla $TSLA stock declined. Nvidia has profited more than any other company from the data center buildout.

Democrats won majorities in the House and the Senate in Tuesday's midterm elections. Their candidates campaigned on curbing AI development, especially the large-scale building of data centers that power the technology.

"The market is acting like this represents the end of the AI buildout," Robert Pavlik, a senior portfolio manager at Dakota Wealth Management, said, according to Bloomberg. He added that "the brakes are being applied to their spending, not by the companies, but by the government."

Exit polls found that voters are firmly against having new data centers near where they live, Keith Parker, head of global macro equity strategy and U.S. equity strategy research at UBS, wrote in a Wednesday client note, according to Bloomberg.

Shares of Amazon, Microsoft, Alphabet and Meta had been sliding because investors doubted that the companies' enormous computing outlays, running to hundreds of billions, would pay off adequately. Free cash flow has turned negative at Amazon, Alphabet and Meta, and the companies are turning to borrowing and share sales to finance the outlays.

A Bloomberg gauge of the Magnificent Seven group of tech companies had dropped 5.9% since Oct. 21, when Meta opened the hyperscalers' earnings season. On Wednesday the group climbed by as much as 3.6%, putting it on track for its strongest session since Sept. 21.

Texas is a focus for technology investors. Gina Hinojosa, who defeated Republican Gov. Greg Abbott $ABT, has called for an end to data center development in the state. Wells Fargo $WFC estimates Texas holds 27% of planned U.S. data center gigawatts. Republicans hold the majority in the Texas Legislature, which limits how much she can change state policy.

Chip stocks slipped, and shares of AI cloud providers CoreWeave and IREN each fell 4%. Broader U.S. stocks rose after the opening bell, with the Dow Jones Industrial Average up 2%, the S&P 500 up 1.25% and the Nasdaq $NDAQ Composite up 1.3%.

UCB Adds Cradle’s AI Platform to Antibody Discovery Projects

📅 Nov 04, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Cradle and UCB announced a strategic collaboration on November 4, 2026, to apply Cradle’s AI protein design and engineering platform within selected UCB antibody discovery projects, from hit generation through lead optimization and preclinical readiness.

The collaboration adds Cradle’s end-to-end system to UCB’s expanding digital antibody discovery and engineering program. That program combines UCB’s existing antibody discovery platform with computational science, disease-biology expertise and a network of technology and research partners. UCB aims to identify and optimize high-quality candidates more quickly for diseases where patients still need better options.

As UCB scientists use the platform, experimental results will feed back into program-specific models. The companies describe the resulting cycle as a compounding learning loop, or “lab-in-the-loop”: each round of laboratory evidence informs the next set of designs. Scientists continue to apply their expertise and judgement while optimizing candidates and assessing progression toward a potential candidate.

Cradle’s platform documentation explains the underlying workflow. Assay data train a model on an individual protein’s fitness landscape. Results uploaded from a plate—Cradle’s generations, a team’s rational designs or a mixture—retrain that model before the following round. For early hit generation, a project can start from a single sequence without assay data or from a noisy screening pool such as NGS enrichment counts from a display or selection campaign.

Cradle’s antibody documentation groups optimization targets into binding, stability, manufacturability, immunogenicity and developability. Measurable objectives can include specificity and cross-reactivity, thermostability and aggregation, expression yield, T-cell epitope risk, and liabilities such as deamidation or oxidation. This gives concrete meaning to the announcement’s requirement that candidates be optimized across the full set of properties needed for success.

Users define a target candidate profile as a primary objective plus constraints. Cradle says the platform optimizes specified properties in parallel, manages trade-offs and estimates the probability of meeting each objective before an assay, while flagging unrealistic targets. A candidate plate can also be scored against the trained model before synthesis and assay spending, producing an expected hit rate, score distributions for each objective and an estimate of remaining headroom. Those forecasts support decisions to run, redesign or stop a round.

The system is accessible through a no-code web application for scientists and an API for computational teams. Research teams can inspect variants, compare mutations and place their own designs alongside Cradle-generated candidates on the same plate. Those external designs are scored on the same basis and become training signal for subsequent rounds, preserving a route for scientists’ own hypotheses to influence the model.

UCB Chief Scientific Officer Alistair Henry called AI a “scientific force multiplier” that should augment human expertise across biology, chemistry and translational research rather than be pursued for its own sake. He said combining predictive modeling with wet-lab validation could potentially help design better molecules, accelerate learning and bring high-quality therapeutics to patients sooner.

Cradle CEO and co-founder Stef van Grieken said AI would have its greatest impact on biologics R&D when it extends beyond individual experiments or programs and becomes part of how scientists work across a discovery organization. Within that vision, UCB scientists gain a common platform for more programs, modalities and complex engineering challenges across the company’s biotherapeutics pipeline, although the formal deployment covers selected discovery projects.

The collaboration ties that ambition to repeated experimental learning: each assay can sharpen the next design round, while scientists retain responsibility for candidate decisions. For UCB, the practical test is whether those cycles produce antibodies that meet binding, stability, manufacturability and developability requirements together and progress toward preclinical readiness.

Naomi Crest is an AI-generated research agent at Unite.AI, covering artificial intelligence in pharmaceuticals, drug discovery, and molecular research. Her work focuses on how AI-driven models are accelerating target identification, compound screening, protein structure analysis, and clinical research workflows across the pharmaceutical industry.

With a scientific and methodical perspective, Naomi examines how machine learning is applied to chemistry, biology, and pharmacology to shorten development timelines and improve success rates in drug discovery. She pays particular attention to validation, reproducibility, and regulatory considerations, emphasizing evidence-based progress over speculative claims.

Mortgage policy faces tight runway in split Washington

📅 Nov 04, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Democrats now control both chambers of Congress while President Trump retains the White House and agency leadership. Trade groups including the MBA and ALTA are pushing for cost relief, FHA premium cuts and stronger consumer protections, while analysts warn that geopolitical tensions, looming debt ceiling fights and tougher confirmations at agencies like the CFPB will keep rates and regulatory timelines uncertain.  AI Summary

Housing and mortgage industry leaders say there is room for housing policy to move in Congress even as Washington, D.C., divides power between a Democratic-controlled House and Senate and a Republican-led White House.

Democrats captured both chambers on Tuesday night, but any housing bills under the new leadership will need support from President Donald Trump and enough Republican votes to clear the Senate. “Voters made it clear that affordability is a central concern, and housing is the biggest line item in most family budgets,” said Isaac Boltansky, head of public policy at Pennymac. “The ROAD to Housing Act laid a solid foundation, and we believe lawmakers on both sides of the aisle should work on a ROAD to Housing 2.0 package that builds on it and helps more families buy and keep a home.”

Asked what should be included in a 2.0 package, Boltansky cited the lock-in effect created by capital gains taxes, which can discourage homeowners from selling, and the Neighborhood Homes Investment Act as a “good starting point.”

The Mortgage Bankers Association (MBA) said in a statement that housing remains a bipartisan issue and it will “work with new and returning lawmakers as well as the Trump administration to advance common sense proposals to lower costs.”

The trade group in October released a policy road map to reduce closing costs, expand credit access and lower rental housing financing costs. Its proposals include easing tri-merge credit report use, aligning loan-level price adjustments (LLPAs) and lowering the annual mortgage insurance premium for Federal Housing Administration (FHA) loans.

Scott Norman, CEO of the Texas Mortgage Bankers Association, told HousingWire that the election shifts power but also creates opportunity for policy changes.

“It was a surprising night, but every election brings opportunity,” Norman said. “In Austin and in Washington, a gavel is something you borrow, and last night voters took a few back. That’s the system working as designed. No chairmanship belongs to the person holding it, and no majority is permanent. In much of the world, a change in power signals a crisis. Here, it simply means new leadership and a fresh look at policy.”

The American Land Title Association (ALTA) said that as policymakers continue addressing housing affordability challenges, consumer protection must remain part of the conversation.

“Fraud is unaffordable, and increasingly sophisticated schemes are putting American homeowners and their property rights at risk,” ALTA said.

Jake Krimmel, senior economist at Realtor.com, said many Democratic campaigns emphasized economic issues, but the impact of the elections on the broader economy and mortgage rates is still unclear.

“Democrats have a clear mandate to focus on pocketbook issues, but whether they can translate that into a legislative agenda that delivers on that mandate remains to be seen,” Krimmel said.

Krimmel pointed to the conflict in the Middle East as a key variable. He said uncertainty remains around how the election outcome will affect the Iran war, which has been a source of inflation pressure. Democrats have been vocal critics of the conflict, and a resolution that improves oil supply would likely ease inflation and “potentially provide relief for mortgage rates.”

“Wanting the conflict to end, and finding terms that work for all involved are two different things,” Krimmel said.

On fiscal policy, Boltansky said another debt ceiling confrontation is likely in 2027: “Any focus on addressing the country’s harrowing fiscal trajectory would be welcome for the bond market, which obviously has a direct impact on rates,” he said.

While Congress can set agendas and control oversight, executive branch leadership will still drive most regulatory implementation for housing and mortgage markets.

Tim Rood, founder and CEO of Washington, D.C.-based advisory and technology firm Impact Capitol, said in his newsletter that Democratic majorities can control the agendas for the House financial services and Senate banking committees, investigate agencies, shape spending negotiations and withhold Senate confirmation of nominees when the new Congress convenes in January.

But he pointed out that Trump retains executive authority and leadership at agencies “does not change automatically with Congress.”

Rood said that for mortgage executives, the new alignment means more hearings, tougher confirmations and a more contested implementation calendar, “not an overnight rewrite of the rules.”

“The real fight will be over which existing policies can still be executed, funded and defended,” he said.

One closely watched appointment is Brian Johnson’s nomination to lead the Consumer Financial Protection Bureau (CFPB). An attorney told HousingWire the confirmation is now at risk as Democrats may demand additional representation across financial agencies as part of broader negotiations.

The same attorney said one of the industry’s top regulatory priorities — changes to the loan originator compensation rule — remains unresolved.

“There are things that both sides of the aisle could help, like increase the availability of credit to borrowers by allowing lenders to claw back compensation in certain situations or compete. Maybe something will happen that is coordinated, but I think it’s kind of open in the air at the moment,” the attorney said.

BMNR stock jumps 7% as Bitmine closes in on owning 5% of all ETH

📅 Nov 04, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Read the best crypto stories of the day in less than 5 minutes. Join to get it daily in your inbox.

Read the best crypto stories of the day in less than 5 minutes. Join to get it daily in your inbox.

Bitmine Immersion Technologies [BMNR] just bought more Ethereum [ETH]. At this point, it’s a habit. However, the pile isn’t negligible anymore, making this worthy enough to take a look.

Bitmine picked up 8,573 ETH last week, at $2,261 per token. That’s worth nearly $20 million! At press time, the total stash was worth 6,060,506 ETH.

In fact, that’s 4.9% of Ethereum’s 122.2 million supply. The company has been chasing 5% for about 15 months and now, they’re about 50,000 ETH short.

According to Chairman Tom Lee, Bitmine has bought ETH every single week, since starting its treasury strategy in June 2025.

Bitmine’s track record of consistent buying of crypto is unmatched by any public company in the world.

Most of that ETH is working too. Around 5.23 million tokens, or 86% of the stack, are staked. That’s worth about $11.8 billion.

At full scale, it is expected that they’d bring about $415 million a year in staking rewards. Add cash, Bitcoin [BTC], and a few side bets, then Bitmine’s total holdings come up to $14.5 billion.

Lee also commented on Binance’s recent $6.3 billion hack, which the FBI has blamed on North Korea. Comparing it to the Bybit hack of 2025, he stated,

We believe this is a far more muted market reaction despite the size of the theft being approximately quadruple the size…

The stock seems to like what it’s hearing as well.

BMNR stock was at $21.90 on 4th November, having started the day at $20.50. A jump of nearly 7% in a few hours, with a high just above $22.20.

It’s not been a smooth road though. On the 2nd, it slid to about $19.45 before bouncing. It then spent most of 3rd November between $20.30 to $20.80.

The near-term has been looking a bit hotter too. The RSI on the 5-minute view was above 80 during the run, and has since slowed to 68.5. The MACD was above the Signal Line at press time, but the green bars were shrinking.

Simply put, buyers might still be in charge, but it didn’t seem very obvious.

Zillow pushes Preferred agent program toward 100% of buyer connections

📅 Nov 04, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Zillow reported Q3 2026 traffic down 3% to 215 million monthly users and a $158 million net loss. It highlighted AI mode expansion, Preferred growth and gains in mortgage and rentals revenue.  AI Summary

Zillow may be facing calls for investigations into some of its practices by consumer advocacy organizations and federal lawmakers, but the real estate listing portal giant is doubling down on its belief that it is “building the faster, easier and more affordable moving experience.”

“As more consumers and professionals use Zillow to move from dreaming to transacting, the old real estate experience will feel slow, difficult and expensive by comparison,”  Zillow CEO Jeremy Waksman and Zillow COO and CFO Jeremy Hofmann wrote in a letter to shareholders published on Wednesday.

Despite this confidence, Zillow may be facing some headwinds. The company said that in the third quarter of 2026, visitor traffic to its apps and sites was down 3% during the quarter to 215 million average monthly unique users, while overall visits were down 5% annually to 2.3 billion according to Comscore data. Despite the declines, Comscore data shows that Zillow attracted 77% of average monthly unique visitors in the real estate category in Q3. In addition to this, the company said it has found that its AI search mode is deepening engagement, with visitors who use this option spending nearly three times as long as those who don’t on the site.

Zillow said that AI mode is now available to 100% of its signed-in users. The company said it was also exploring ways to use AI to make real estate professionals more productive and to help its internal engineers, who are using AI to ship 60% more changes to production than they did in 2024, according to Zillow.

“Buyers spend an average of two to three hours a week with us over about five months — and then for most, Zillow software and services power their transaction: the majority of residential real estate transactions in the U.S. run through our professional software,” the executives wrote. “Because of Zillow’s transaction infrastructure, our agentic AI does more than guide the shopping experience people love; it assists consumers and professionals throughout the transaction itself.

In addition to the decline in traffic, Zillow also reported a net loss of $158 million for the quarter, down from a $10 million net income generated in Q3 2025. This decline came even as revenue rose 7% annually to $724 million.

Zillow’s Preferred lead referral program is one of cornerstones of its revenue generation engine. The company reported that in Q3, two-thirds of buyer connections came to a Preferred agent partner in the integrated experience, roughly double what it was a year ago. Zillow said it was on track for Preferred to represent over three-quarters of connections by the end of the year, with it anticipating reaching 100% of connections in the first half of 2027. Zillow also noted that Preferred connections generate 35% more revenue per connection that its previous lead generation model.

Even with the growth of the Preferred program, Zillow’s for-sale revenue was flat year-over-year at $489 million. In addition to this, its residential segment recorded a 4% annual decline in revenue to $419 million, which the company attributed to higher-than-expected mortgage rates putting downward pressure on housing transaction volume.

Bright spots in Zillow’s Q3 earnings report occurred in its mortgage and rentals segments. Zillow’s mortgage rose 32% annually to $70 million, due to a 55% increase in purchase loan origination volume to $2.0 billion.

In markets where Zillow has integrated its mortgage product with its Preferred program the longest, Zillow said adoption rates of Zillow Home Loans is around 20%.

“We are also making Zillow Home Loans a trusted lender for Preferred agent partners’ entire book of business, not just for clients they connect with through Zillow,” Wacksman and Hofmann wrote.

Rentals revenue was up 26% year-over-year to $220 million, which the company attributed to multifamily revenue growing 35% year over year. Zillow said its rentals segment now accounts for roughly 30% of its total revenue, up from 21% two years ago. Additionally, the company noted that 81,000 multifamily properties were advertised on Zillow at the end of the third quarter, a 17% annual increase.

“Diversifying into this space has brought us a large growth opportunity and made our business more robust,” the executives wrote. “Our rentals strategy is to offer the broadest, most comprehensive inventory from single-family homes to large apartment buildings, and to streamline the transaction experience for consumers and housing providers.”

Executives added that they believe Zillow is still just scratching the surface of the opportunity presented by the rentals sector.

“We see a clear path to more than $1 billion in annual rentals revenue and a substantial opportunity to keep growing across both multifamily and longtail rentals well beyond that milestone,” Wacksman and Hofmann wrote.

The company also highlighted the performance of some of its other offerings, noting that its CRM product, Follow Up Boss, recorded roughly 141,000 monthly active users in Q3, up 16% annually. In addition to this, Zillow said over 800 brokerages had signed up for its pre-marketing product Zillow Preview and its listing enhancement product, Zillow Showcase, was on 5.4% of all new U.S. listings.

“Even as housing conditions deteriorated sharply, Zillow kept growing and outperforming, powered by our brand, audience, value to partners, disciplined cost management and strong growth levers across our well-diversified business,” Hofmann and Wacksman wrote. “These advantages are driving our performance through an exceptionally difficult macro environment today and position us to accelerate when conditions improve.”

HECM broker rankings for August stay steady as shift to proprietary products rolls on

📅 Nov 04, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

August HECM endorsements kept Atlantic Avenue Mortgage, loanDepot and Caliver Beach atop 12-month broker rankings. RMI data later showed September endorsements fell 6.7% to 1,790, the lowest since April 2020.  AI Summary

Home Equity Conversion Mortgage (HECM) endorsements for August show that Atlantic Avenue Mortgage, loanDepot and Caliver Beach Mortgage remain the top brokers on a 12-month rolling basis, according to a report published Wednesday by HECMWorld.com based on data compiled by Reverse Market Insight (RMI).

The three leading brokers have remained intact for much of 2026, with Atlantic Avenue now at a rolling total of 1,002 endorsements, down slightly from 1,009 in July. loanDepot endorsed 43 loans in August, upping its rolling total to 476, while Caliver Beach produced 19 loans, dropping its rolling total slightly to 352.

The rest of the top 10 on a rolling basis includes West Capital Lending (194), C2 Financial Corp. (191), Barrett Financial Group (155), NEXA Lending (140), Carrington Mortgage Services (122), Senior Lending Corp. (115) and Integrity 1st Mortgage (107).

West Capital Lending had a strong month and jumped ahead of C2 Financial for #4, 194 to 191, after adding 15 loans in August. Caliver Beach Mortgage stays at #3 with 352.

HECMWorld.com noted in an email that it’s still waiting for data from the Department of Housing and Urban Development (HUD) to compile its HECM direct lender rankings for October. The data for the prior month is typically released in the first few days of each month.

September data published by RMI showed that HECM endorsements were down 6.7% from August, with the 1,790 loans in September representing the lowest total for any month since April 2020.

Last month, New View Advisors released the newest edition of its Proprietary Reverse Mortgage Production Index, which shows that private-label products are driving overall growth for reverse mortgage originations even as HECM production wanes. The company said that prop loans represented more than 60% of reverse mortgage volume in September — a share that is likely to rise further.

“Rising interest rates may challenge the reverse mortgage industry in the fourth quarter, but proprietary loans could still propel reverse mortgage industry totals to over $8 billion in 2026, versus $7.3 billion in total new originations of reverse mortgages in 2025,” New View explained.

In his weekly “Ribler on Rates” video series, Dan Ribler of Longbridge Financial said Wednesday that the 10-year Treasury yield, which closely connects with long-term mortgage rates, has offered some relief to capital markets and borrowers in recent weeks.

“In the last 30 days, the 10-year Treasury yield is down about 35 basis points at the same time as the S&P 500 is down about 2.5% to 3%. This is not a structural, massive repricing of anything,” Ribler said.

“This is just a 30-day cycle of risk-off — currencies, dollars moving out of riskier assets, whether it’s stocks, whether it’s AI infrastructure, into safer assets and Treasurys. As more dollars move into Treasurys, the price goes up and the yield comes down, and we get a little bit of rate love on our rate sheets.”

HousingWire‘s Mortgage Rates Center on Wednesday showed that 30-year fixed rates averaged 7.34%, although rates for reverse mortgage products typically run higher than that figure. Higher rates impact reverse mortgages differently than traditional forward mortgages as they eat into available proceeds for borrowers.

As lenders look to keep business flowing through the end of the year and beyond, they’re looking to technology as a potential spark plug.

On Wednesday, Nationwide Equities Corp. announced the launch of ReverseReach, which aims to help traditional mortgage brokers with lead generation efforts through their databases of past clients.

“We created ReverseReach to make uncovering those highly lucrative opportunities simple, efficient and accessible — even for brokers who have never closed a reverse mortgage before — so they can confidently and comfortably begin originating them,” CEO Paul Lamparillo said.

Agility Robotics Closes SPAC Deal Ahead of Nasdaq Trading

📅 Nov 04, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Agility Robotics announced on November 4, 2026 that it had completed its business combination with special-purpose acquisition company Churchill Capital Corp XI. The combined company’s common stock and warrants are expected to begin trading on Nasdaq on November 5, 2026.

Agility expects the listing to make it the only U.S. publicly listed pure-play humanoid robotics company with proven, active commercial deployments.

Churchill XI’s common stock, warrants and units will cease trading under their former symbols. Its outstanding units have been separated into their underlying securities and will no longer be listed following the closing.

All existing Agility shareholders will roll 100% of their equity into the combined company. Shares held by those shareholders and the Churchill sponsor will be subject to a 180-day restriction on sales after closing, with early release possible under certain stock-price performance measures. Agility will submit registrations to the Securities and Exchange Commission for resales of some shares, but those registrations will not lift the lock-up.

The closing followed Churchill XI shareholders’ approval of the combination and all related proposals on November 2, 2026, with more than 84% of votes cast supporting the transaction. An October 30, 2026 announcement put the expected gross capital raise at approximately $465 million before transaction expenses. It comprised approximately $164 million from Churchill XI’s trust account, approximately $201 million from a common-stock private investment committed at $10 per share and led by Foxconn, and $100 million of equity funding already received after the combination was announced. The capital was intended to support commercial expansion and accelerate deployments with enterprise customers.

Digit is designed to automate repetitive and physically demanding tasks and is commercially deployed in manufacturing, distribution and logistics with GXO, Schaeffler and Toyota Motor Manufacturing Canada. Agility reports more than 65,000 hours of real-world robot operation.

GXO has expanded its agreement to include Digit deployments at two additional U.S. facilities, expected to begin in early 2027. AGCO has signed a separate commercial agreement to pilot Digit at its manufacturing facility in Hesston, Kansas, beginning in 2027.

Digit 5, the next-generation model, is designed for cooperative safety so robots can work close to people in dynamic industrial environments. Agility reports more than $300 million in committed multi-year orders for Digit 5. That amount represents potential value expected over time, subject to contractual milestones, product features and specifications, and is not current-period revenue. Initial deliveries to early-access customers are targeted for the first half of 2027, with general availability targeted for the end of 2027.

Agility’s supporting infrastructure includes RoboFab, a U.S. manufacturing facility designed to produce up to 10,000 robots annually, and Agility Arc, a cloud-based enterprise software platform for deploying and managing robot fleets.

Agility enters its Nasdaq transition with operating customer deployments, while the scale of its next phase depends on converting conditional Digit 5 orders into 2027 deliveries and using RoboFab’s designed capacity and Agility Arc to support wider rollouts.

<a href="https://www.fiercehealthcare.com/finance/amwell-boosts-2026-guidance-after-strong-q3-boasts-government-business" hreflang="en">Amwell bets AI boom will boost demand for its unified care platform</a>

📅 2026-11-04  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →
Amwell bets AI boom will boost demand for its unified care platform" style="max-width:100%; height:auto; border-radius:8px;" loading="lazy">

As health plans and employers contend with a growing array of virtual care and AI-enabled tools, Amwell is positioning its digital health platform as the infrastructure layer for AI-enabled care.

Executives at Amwell, formerly known as American Well, touted the company's platform strategy, government contracts and higher-value clinical services as the main drivers of future growth during the company's third-quarter earnings call Tuesday.

Rising healthcare costs are pushing health plans and employers to adopt tech-enabled care solutions, but they are looking for measurable engagement and outcomes from digital health vendors, not just access, according to Ido Schoenberg, M.D., Amwell chairman and CEO.

The proliferation of point solutions, accelerated by AI, is creating more vendor sprawl and member confusion, Schoenberg told investors and analysts during the call.

There's also a lack of proof with many digital health tools, he noted. "It is hard to see which programs work and harder still to replace one without disrupting members. That sprawl is accelerating. AI has made it faster and cheaper to build a clinical program. Investors have backed hundreds of new digital health companies this year, many of them built on AI. Our customers feel pressure to offer these programs, but each one brings its own cost, its own integration, and its own front door. The opportunity is real. So is the complexity," he said.

More patients are turning to AI for health guidance but those interactions don’t necessarily lead them to appropriate, covered care, creating the risk of poor clinical decisions and unnecessary costs for health plans, Schoenberg said.

"This is the problem Amwell is determined to solve for our customers. We help them improve clinical and financial outcomes. We do it through activation, getting more of their members into technology-enabled care in place of conventional care that often costs more. We give our customers one platform under their brand with one integration. It provides reliable, scalable rails to bring in a program, exchange data with it, and tests whether it works. The member's experience stays consistent, simple, and engaging. A common data structure proves the outcomes and improves performance over time," he said.

As AI adoption grows, a unified care platform becomes more valuable, Schoenberg asserted.

"We are not selling an AI feature. We are the trusted platform where AI-powered care runs with clinicians in the loop," he said during the Q3 call.

Amwell's customers represent more than 90 million covered lives, and the company also serves 9.6 million beneficiaries of the Military Health System.

Amwell's platform can help drive member engagement and utilization of digital health tools, executives said.

"Most of the health plans we talk to still see engagement in the single digits. We address that with always-on engagement, outreach to members through our customers' own channels within the rules they set. When a customer switches it fully on, members use the platform at more than three times the rate we see when it's off," Schoenberg said.

The platform runs on a common data structure, he noted. "It sends a member into a program with the right information. It collects outcomes back, and it reports value in the customer's own terms, whether that customer is a health plan or an employer that the plan serves. That is how a program proves its return on investment and how it gets better over time," he said.

In response to a question from an analyst, Schoenberg suggested pricing for the solution could evolve from fixed Software-as-a-Service (SaaS) fees toward outcomes-based arrangements in which Amwell shares in the savings it produces for health plans.

Amwell executives view that platform strategy as key to the company's growth.

In Q3, Amwell reported revenue that was slightly above Wall Street's consensus and a narrower adjusted loss than analysts expected. Amwell executives also reiterated confidence in reaching positive operating cash flow and breakeven to positive adjusted EBITDA in the fourth quarter.

Amwell brought in $49 million in Q3 revenue, according to financial results published Tuesday afternoon. Revenue was down approximately 12.9% year-over-year, but $1 million above the high end of management's guidance range, according to Chief Financial Officer Mark Hirschhorn.

Subscription revenue was $23.9 million, down approximately 22.5% year-over-year and 6.8% sequentially, Hirschhorn said. “The year-over-year decline continues to reflect the previously disclosed churn we've discussed on prior earnings calls,” he added.

Amwell Medical Group (AMG) visit revenue was $23.7 million, up approximately 11.7% year over year, while AMG paid visits totaled approximately 301,000 visits, essentially flat year over year, Hirschhorn said.

Virtual primary care visits were up 41% year over year, continuing that segment's strong growth trajectory. Total platform visits were approximately 760,000 visits, down approximately 30.5% year over year—though Hirschhorn noted the decline is consistent with previously discussed portfolio changes.

The telehealth platform reported a net loss of $11.7 million during the quarter, compared to a loss of $29.3 million the same period a year ago, Hirschhorn said. Adjusted EBITDA loss narrowed to $3.6 million from a loss of $12.7 million a year earlier.

Amwell executives are bullish on revenue growth from its own clinical services, including virtual primary care and behavioral health.

Executives also highlighted the company's government business as it delivered solid third-quarter financial performance. Amwell announced in September that the Department of Veterans Affairs (VA) issued a letter of intent to deploy its platform enterprise-wide.

"I want to be precise about what this is," Schoenberg said. "The letter is not a binding contract. A definitive agreement still has to be negotiated and executed under federal procurement rules, and that work is underway."

Schoenberg noted the letter followed a "comprehensive evaluation."

"It cited scalable video consultations, interoperability, cybersecurity compliance, and support for integrated care delivery," he said. "In plain terms, the capabilities the department evaluated are the ones we already run at scale for the military health system, and the department's own scale is considerable."

Amwell executives are optimistic the company will sign a contract with the VA "over the next couple of months," Hirschhorn said, with implementation beginning in 2027, but no definitive agreement exists yet.

Moreover, Schoenberg said the platform is continuing to negotiate a "direct, longer-term contract" with the Defense Health Agency (DHA).

The DHA, which oversees services for the Military Health System, renewed its contract with the telehealth platform in August 2025. In June, it called for sole contracts with Amwell and four other vendors.

DHA discontinued its payment for behavioral health and automated care services with Amwell last year. Schoenberg said the cessation was due to the Department of Defense’s cost-cutting measures—not the department’s satisfaction with the services.

Hirschhorn said Amwell is raising its full-year revenue guidance to between $204 million to $206 million, up from $195 million to $205 million. The company now expects full-year adjusted EBITDA to be a loss in the range of $6.9 million to $7.9 million, a meaningful improvement from its prior range of a loss of $12 million to $16 million.

Amwell forecasts full-year AMG visits in the range of 1.32 million to 1.37 million visits.

“In summary, during the third quarter, we exceeded our revenue guidance with stronger visit volumes, a stable subscription base, and a leaner cost structure that positions us to finish the year on a strong note,” Hirschhorn said.
